ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e498	It is high time that the Working Environment Act finally came into force in the United Kingdom, not only because the process has been in existence for a year now since the debate began, but since it is soon ten years since the current Labour Environment Act was passed. Today's law is almost ten years away, and I am therefore pleased that I, as the case rapporteur and committee leader today, am able to present a position with broad support in Stortinget. Both the communication and the compromise in Stortinget ensure the tripartite cooperation with which Norway has been blessed. Of course, we have tried, since the government chose to bring forward two issues — the temporary employment and the obligation of activity in relation to the social services law — to deal with this in parallel in the committee. And we've made that clear. I would first like to commend the Committee for a very good cooperation. Not least, I would like to thank our partners in the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left. A well-functioning labour market is essential for each one of us to realize our dreams and ambitions and that it will pay off to work. Working and having a job to go to has changed significantly in recent years. That's why the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left believe that the changes that are going to continue are going to have to be met by a work environment law that looks something different than today. The Labour Environment Act covers a very wide range of employers and employees. The law shall apply to small individual enterprises and to heavy limited liability companies and, in particular, to enterprises with thousands of employees; industrial groups. The law applies in public, private, local, national and international establishments in Norway. This poses a challenge for the Storting, because the same law applies and sets the same rules for all businesses, regardless of size, ownership, national and international competitive conditions, physical or stationary work, services or production. And then we know that an increasingly large part of the labour market is exposed to international competition. Another development that has been going on for a long time, without the Storting ever addressing the problem, is that many people choose to take out the growth we've seen in recent years, differently. For some, it means the growth in income that they want to work even more, since it's profitable. For others, increased income means that they want to work less and have more leisure time, as they now get better advice. Experience in the last two decades suggests that, overall, the population wants to take some of the prosperity development we have had out in more leisure time. It requires the Labour Environment Act to offer increased flexibility to the labour market in order to address precisely these challenges. Demographic projections show that labour shortages are becoming one of the major challenges in Norwegian working life in the future, regardless of the current situation — with somewhat adjusted unemployment. The Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left are followers of a strong labour-environment law, where the main rule is fixed employment, where the working time framework is to be continued, where the flexibility of the workplace is to be greater than today, and where violations of the labour-environment law are to be penalised harder and more stringent than today. The Government Parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left are now fulfilling the commitments to increase the use of working time averages, which will help to resolve the needs of many enterprises for flexibility, as reported. Variations in the number of jobs are common, and employees need to be able to work more for one period, in return for equivalent freedom in other periods. The cooperation parties believe that the use of a long-term watch could help to increase the number of full-time employees and increase the number of employees. A number of jobs using this today report an increase in service quality, while a number of jobs report a central rejection of the renewal of these tour arrangements by trade unions. Local trustees together with employees and employers will, in our view, be able to find good solutions to tournament arrangements, as they do today, without going about Oslo and the trade unions centrally. If there is doubt or disagreement, this may be agreed directly with the Labour Inspectorate or the Petroleum Inspectorate. Now there is also an embellishment about Sunday work, i.e. you can work more Sundays in a row than today. It will benefit the travel industry, it will benefit the restaurant industry, it will benefit young people, but not least it will benefit those who do not want to work on Sundays, because more people who want it will have the opportunity to work more, as well as those who do not want to work more, will have the opportunity to get free – in other words, that there will be an average calculation for Sunday work where the framework is maintained, but where the number of Sundays in a row can be worked is increased. That means an increase in flexibility. Life expectancy for men at 65 years of age has increased from 79,5 years in 1986 to 83.4 years in 2013. Life expectancy for women increased from 83.7 years to 86.2 years in the same period. There is an increase in the remaining life span of almost 4 years for men and 2.5 years for women. The average age at which economically active people can expect to leave the labour market has been increasing, from 61 in 2003 to 65 in 2014. And I'm pretty sure this, with the changes the size of today, will rise in the future. An increasing proportion of the active population can expect to wait to leave the labour market until after they have reached the age of 67. The share that continues to work shows research, increases by 20–50 pst. when the age limit increases by one year. Raising the age limit in the Working Environment Act will make it easier for those who want to work even after the age of 70. I do not see any serious reasons why 70-year-olds are going to lose the protection against dismissal they currently have in the Working Environment Act. I believe it is wasted on human resources and I am therefore committed to limiting access to lower occupational age limits than 70 years to cases where this is only necessary for health or safety reasons. There I am pleased that the Center Party has broken out of the red-green cooperation and supported the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left. The cooperation parties are associated with tripartite cooperation between the country, the employee organisations and the employers’ organisations. The right is dependent on the organisation of working life in Norway. Organised employees through trade unions and organised enterprises through employers' associations contribute to the stability, structure and seriousness of business. That's why the changes we've made, largely favored the organized working life to a greater extent than the unorganized one. It is part of the entire package that Stortinget will now take a decision on. But of course, the Right is also a member of the Freedom of Association. It's the individual human being and the individual enterprise that has to decide for themselves whether you're earned membership. The cooperation parties agree with the government on a number of other proposals, including: Today, it is decided to remove collective redress, as proposed by the government, and as promised in the election campaign. It's a system that's not used very well, that has legal challenges with it, and that we're now proposing to cancel. In addition, the government has received full support from all parties in this room to increase the penalties for breaches of the labour protection law and the Public Service Act. We provide significant resources for research in Norwegian working life, and we provide increased resources to the Labour Inspectorate in order to ensure good and rapid processing as a result of new tasks and responsibilities, not only in this law, but also in the matter that comes next. Collaborative parties strengthen the work between relevant supervision to improve the information available on alerts. We're going to strengthen alerts legislation, there's going to be a common guide with information about rights and obligations for all the parties involved, there's going to be an information campaign on alerts in cooperation with the social partners, we're going to suggest that alert procedures should become a mandatory requirement and a minimum content of these procedures should be clarified. In conclusion, I would like to thank the Cooperation Committee again, and not least Committee Secretary Berit Skovly and Senior Consultant Ingunn Oppsahl Viered for good work. We have been able to deliver quite well from us over three dense weeks of treatment. As I have seen the situation, the Committee has been satisfied with the way in which the work of the Committee has been carried out. The size will today adopt a forward-looking work environment law that will help to equip Norwegian working life for the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e642	The Labour Party is concerned that we should have a world-class working life, where the policy facilitates high employment, high productivity, high levels of innovation and adaptability, and that we should have a working life where both women and men and those who are 100 pst. fresh, and non-participating. We're concerned that our job should be a good place to be, where we have influence, where we get to evolve, where it's a short way from head to employee, and where people feel security and thus create more. The Working Environment Act is only a small part of the policy that creates the fine machinery of the Norwegian working life model. The law has been amended 50 times. I heard the representative Kambe say that today's law is ten years old. That's not right. Most of the time, the Working Environment Act has been amended by the Labour Party — always in line with the needs and challenges of work and society. We changed it by 2013 at the latest, when we introduced increased entitlement to full-time work, in order to overcome involuntary part-time work. And we put in place new, powerful instruments to combat work-life crime. It's because there's two of the biggest challenges in the world of work and society. It was modern and necessary changes. I guess the government parties also have an ambition to preserve and develop a world-class working life, and they're also trying to set up policies to that end. But it seems that today's government is weakening the Working Environment Act because it's an objective in itself, because the changes we're going to adopt here tonight don't reflect in any way the reality of working life, the challenges or the everyday problems that most people experience on their bodies. What is the reality of working life, and what challenges do we have to solve? Yes, we have to get more people to work – regular work. The combination of working life and family life must be easier and it must be more difficult to be unprofessional and work-life criminals. Then we need work time regulations that ensure people's health, so that they can stand out in work, that ensure that both women and men can combine having family and children with working, and that people who are not 100 pst. the whole time, also gets one place and not squeezed out. And we need more powerful measures against social dumping and labour crime. If the government were concerned about meeting these challenges, they should have made proposals today for changes to the Working Environment Act that made it easier to meet those challenges. Is that what the government is doing? No, it doesn't. On the contrary, the government is looking at professional advice that says that the changes in total can lead to increased sick leave, more exclusion, that it may be more difficult for people with disabilities to get into work and to go to work, and that more women will choose part-time to be able to hang out with. That's what the Labour Inspectorate says, that's what the State Institute for Safety and Health at Work says, that's what the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman says. And a collective employee page, which organizes millions of workers, went to the general strike against the proposals. This is because longer shifts, more overtime and more weekends as a whole increase the risk of health problems, accidents, sick leave and exclusion – the plight of the opposite of working life and ordinary people in very common occupations, with heavy lifting or many patients, now needs. The proposals could make it more difficult for many people to combine family life with working life and to strengthen the time frame, which we are dedicated to solving. None of the small children's parents I know, who are struggling to get the cabal with houses, homes, jobs and leisure activities to go up, say that only they got to work a little longer guards, a little more overtime and a little more weekends, then it would be much easier to get the cabal to go up. Nobody says that. Norway has a higher level of female employment than other countries. This is because of, and not despite, the working time rules. It also says the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman and an overall employee page. It's these rules that government parties are now moving in the wrong direction. It's the true opposite of the working life and the common people who are struggling with the time clamp, now need. The government's proposals will make it more difficult to combat social dumping and crime at work. We have a reality where people are underpaid and treated in the most unworthy way. Serious actors in many industries are being wiped out. We have almost weekly examples of this happening – despite the strong efforts of the red-green government. Then we need more powerful measures against social dumping and crime at work. Then we need the policy to facilitate more transparent conditions, more fixed employment and fewer loopholes for the unprofessional. Does the government's changes do that? No, on the contrary. We are used to the fact that the government parties and the right-hand side do not listen to the Workers' Party, but on the contrary our proposals for action against social dumping and labour-life-criminalism are opposed – not later than Christmas, when we proposed 22 new ones. We're used to that. However, they also do not choose to listen to the Labour Inspectorate and Kripos, who warn that changes by the government will make it difficult to combat just work-life crime serious. More use of temporary employment, less equal treatment of hired and permanent workers and the removal of social dumping measures will make it easier, not more difficult, to be unprofessional — the very opposite of what the labour market now needs. It is the very opposite of the construction worker from Lithuania, who is underpaid and not afraid to say because he is dependent on income, and who sees that his proud trades are in danger of confusion, needs now. That's the very opposite of all of the serious Norwegian companies that are going to go bankrupt because it's impossible to run seriously, now needs to. The changes we are making here today will make it harder to meet all these challenges. People see that, and that's why people are against it. Its own trade unions and supervisory bodies are also negative. Nevertheless, government parties continue to pretend that changes are the best thing for people's everyday lives, and that they will have a positive impact on employment, sick leave, time lag and crime at work. We've seen it before. The same batches claimed that the right of reservation was for the benefit of women themselves. The victim would profit from being deprived of social security. The Community would serve to sell away from natural resources. Once again, the government parties claim to know people better than they do, and rely on the government's brother-in-law's research from Verdal. Many ask why it is so dangerous to expand the average calculation – most people have tipped the top of the job and a cool boss! That's right. Norwegian employment consists largely of serious enterprises and employers who would never like to cross the line and treat their employees badly. Yes, most of the employers I'm talking to are so serious that they really didn't need any work environment laws to behave properly, but that's not the whole picture of what Norwegian working life looks like. The employers who are not such do not organise and do not attend our hearings. We need a work environment law that innovates that some people need flexibility, while others need more protection — hence, the Labour Environment Act precisely emphasises the need for flexibility and protection. Unlike the Right and Advance Party, we take inwards that it is not that the employee and the employer are equivalent parties. Then we can't have a law like that later on. An employee of a car wash where the employer presses can use the legal framework as an emergency brake, while an employee of an IT company, who wants to work a lot, can agree to it. This is how the law works today. People are different and have different needs – the law takes that into account. Enterprises are different and have different needs — the law takes this into account as well. The government's proposed changes do not offer any new opportunities for the type of flexibility and no new benefits for workers they do not already have through the contract work – none. On the contrary, they weaken the protection of those who already feel the pressure. In addition, the law today makes it more difficult to understand, use and enforce by introducing complex rules and discrimination against temporary staff. It is also the true opposite of the Norwegian working life now needed. The working party is too flexible for those who want it, and we're too protective for those who need it. We need changes in the Working Environment Act that make it easier to get people into permanent jobs and to keep people in regular jobs, easier to combine family life with working life and more difficult to be unprofessional. It is the needs of the labour force and society. The government's proposal for amendments, which we are going to adopt here today, is immature. These are weaknesses and old ideas that you've tried before and that don't work. In total, changes make it more difficult to meet the challenges of working life and the needs of society. That is why the Labour Party is against it.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e696	Under the legislation of today's Working Environment Act, nine out of ten dispensations applications for the approval of alternative turns are approved by central trade unions. But it also happens that central trade unions say no. And there are many cases where local trustees agree with the employer, but leave it to apply, because they know they're going to get a negative answer. Why does the Workers' Party think that central trade unions should have the power and authority to reduce the number of solutions that both employees and employers want locally? And doesn't the Labour Party see that there is something fundamentally problematic in that one of the parties has a central veto right in negotiations on working time that there is agreement on local?
ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e703	It is based on a fundamental misunderstanding that trade unions have a central right to veto and a right to say no to what is agreed locally. What we have said, in the law, and on which there has been a cross-political agreement until today's government took over, is that because the social partners and trade unions are at the heart of tariffs and already negotiate pay and working time issues – tariff agreements can reduce working time less than what the legal framework limits – trade unions can, therefore, centrally set aside the whole law if you want to. It is such that nine out of ten applications are accepted, as the representative here said. It is a flexible system that works well. When we want to keep the system as it is today, that the trade unions are central to making the decision, it is because the trustees locally want it. They say that in those cases where there is no real consensus locally, it's okay to have the central associations to rely on.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e790	Finally, the day is here – a day that I think many have looked forward to. It's basically two big things we're dealing with today. Afterwards, we will deal with a matter that is about helping people into working life, while now we are dealing with a matter of creating more flexibility for those who are in work – we are dealing with a matter that can make it easier to continue to be in the workplace for people who are at risk of falling out of it. Creating flexibility in the workplace is about securing jobs, and it is about having a work environment law that is adapted to modern society. The Labour Environment Act is an important law, which provides for a good and orderly relationship of power between the employee and the employer. But it is also a challenge to create a law that will apply to all types of enterprises, both large and small, in different industries and in different municipalities. It's a challenge, because no people are the same, and no business is the same. It is then important to have flexibility in the Labour Environment Act, so that there is room for discussion in the individual workplace, up to good arrangements adapted to the needs and wishes of both the employer and the employee. Some people try to produce employment in Norway as if there is a high contrast between the employer and the employee. It is a claim that both I and the Progress Party strongly disagree. Because there is something the employers and businesses depend on, there are well-motivated employees. It is the most important resource in each and every enterprise. At the same time, however, employees also depend on having a safe job, and I have confidence in the local discussions in the individual workplaces. The statistics show that nine out of ten workers thrive at work. They believe that good solutions are found in good dialogue between the employer and the employee. I am therefore pleased that the main act we are doing today is transferring power and authority down to those who know where the shoe is pressing, so that we can get customized solutions. Despite the fact that there has been a considerable amount of scare propaganda about this matter, let me make one point: all the changes we are proposing here today, require local consensus. This means that if the employer and the employee agree on changes, changes can be made. If the employer or employee does not wish to change, there will be no change either. I would also like to thank the government and the Council of State very much, which provided a good basis for putting this heavy, big and important law into place and the adjustments and modernisation of it. I would like to thank the employers' and employees' organisations, which have made many good contributions along the way, and I would like to thank the cooperation partners Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left for having demonstrated good flexibility – all parties have given and taken to achieve our common goal, to secure jobs and to provide flexibility. Today's work environment law dates from 1977. It was then a good work environment law in many areas. Then there have been some adjustments, but we have to realize that society in 2015 is different from society in 1977. No human being is an A4 human being. Work life has changed. Fewer and fewer have standardised jobs – eight to four jobs – but all have different needs, abilities and desires. We see that family forms change, we see that more have shared care for their children. I'm glad that with this law, we are better able to make sure that a single mother or a single father who cares for his children every other week, now, if they want to, will be able to work more in the periods they don't have the children, in exchange for more freedom in the periods they have the children. That's an example of some of the flexibility we want to give. Then there has also been a lot of focus on Sunday work. With the changes we are now proposing, it's not about someone working more Sundays than you do today. Not one Sunday more than today we add up to. What we're adding is that you can work a few more Sundays in a row in exchange for free on other Sundays. It can be excellent for people in different situations of life. A student who is at home on vacation and perhaps wants to work three Sundays in a row, will now be allowed to do so. And what happens when those who want to work a few more Sundays in a row, for example. The student, is that allowed? Then those who may not want to work on Sundays can get more out of work on Sundays, because we have now opened up so that the student can do it. During the debate that has taken place in the last year, it has also been presented by some parties as if people can now be forced to work 13 hours of work, and that is not the way. That is incorrect information. In the proposal Stortinget deals with today, there is no increase in overtime imposed, but if the employee and the employer wish to do so locally or local trade unions should be able to work a little more overtime to make ends meet. It's about the basic trust we have in local relationships, not only for the individuals, but also for the local trust leaders – that they know best where the shoe prints. That's also why we move power from Youngstorget down to the local trustee when it comes to the famous right of veto in tournament arrangements. We believe that this is more democratic and will ensure greater flexibility. Then, at the same time, we ensure that we have a safety valve by ensuring that the Labour Inspectorate as a neutral and independent party is to check that the HMS requirements are satisfied, because that is important. However, if the HMS requirements are satisfied, the Progress Party considers it unproblematic to agree locally on other tour arrangements. Another thing that is pleasing to the Progress Party that we are getting through today is about the unworthy treatment of many older workers. The party of progress has long been in favour of raising the age limits in the labour market, because we want individuals to be able to choose for themselves how long they want to stand in the labour market. Those who want to leave between 62 and 67 years of age should still have the opportunity to do so – freely – while those who want to work beyond 70 years should have the opportunity to do so. Many older people might want to work in half jobs, for example, when they round 70, and I don't understand why the opposition in this room is voting against our proposal to strengthen the rights of older workers, and that you want more of the power to be with the employer than with the employee. The party disagrees that older people should wear a date stamp. We believe that it is the ability and the desire of the individual worker to determine the length of working life. Now that we are creating a more flexible labour-environment law, which provides greater room for action locally, it is also important – and I am very pleased about it – that the government is in favour of, and the Storting is now deciding to increase the level of penalties for breaches of the labour-environment law. Because when we have a flexible law, it is also important to follow it. We're going to have zero tolerance to work-life crime, and I'm very happy that where others talked, we're acting, by now increasing the level of penalties for breaches of the labour-environment law. A 1977 Labour Environment Act is being adapted today to the flexibility that many people want in 2015.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e1509	Over 100 years ago, people on demonstration trains went for regulated working time and that day would consist of equal parts of work, sleep and leisure time. In the 1970s, women were on trains to combine work with family life. Now the government and the majority of the population will reverse the clock by weakening the Working Environment Act, which is one of the cornerstones of Norwegian working life and equality. Here in the hall, the right parties have referred to the Working Environment Act as a museum object. That's not right. The Labour Environment Act is a law that has been constantly developed and amended in the course of labour market developments. Since its first adoption in 1977, more than 50 amendments have been made to it, most recently when we adopted the multi-time paragraph, which guarantees important rights for those who work involuntary part-time. And more importantly, the law has been designed to ensure that the good solutions are found in the workplace within a framework that ensures the lives and health of employees. That's good for the individual, and that's wise of society, because if we push people too hard, we risk pushing people out of work and into disability and disease. The Perspective Communication showed that the labour force is our sovereign most important resource, and it underlined the need to take good care of the labour force. Today, we should expect a knowledge-based approach to take account of the changes you are making. The government chooses to rely on the Minister of Labour's brother-in-law's research. What the government is doing now is changing the law, in strong conflict with an overall trade union movement. The Minister of Labour may have taken individual people who believe that the law does not apply to them, but a collective trade union movement – UNIO, YS and LO – representing about 1.5 million members, is opposed to this. The government also acts despite criticism and warnings from all available expertise. According to the Labour Inspectorate, the changes in the Labour Environment Act can lead to more sickness, stress and accidents, and they also warn that the changes in the laws will facilitate more labour market criminality. The Labour Inspectorate refers to figures from the Statistical Central Bureau, which shows that temporary staff stood for about 30 p.m. of all damage to construction that was treated at Trondheim Municipality Medical Care, even if they were only 5 pst. by employees. These higher figures are particularly dramatic because we know that they are also underreported. Nevertheless, the government is capable of producing the legislative changes as moderate and almost as a labour market measure. This is part of a long tradition of the two government parties. The Right Party and the Progressive Party have voted against all the main proposals we have had against social dumping and crime in the labour market. The Right Party and the Progressive Party have always argued – as they do today – that it will close weak groups out of work. Here's a pattern: they're trying to wrap the right-hand side ideology into noble intentions to help someone in, while they're actually pushing people out and contributing to a B team in the world of work. If the government was so concerned about those who are out of work, it is strange that they cut in action places rather than bet more on it. More than 200000 people receive redundancy benefits today. Nav assesses that around 120 000 of these need action sites. The Right Party and the Advance Party have no answer to that question. It's because of a lack of reality. They make it appear that Norwegian working life is rigid, stuck and without ability to change. The truth is the opposite: today we have a flexible system. It's a negotiated flexibility. We don't have job security, but we have job security, and we have a lot of that security thanks to tripartite cooperation, local and central. With its approach, the government is weakening this vital strength of Norwegian employment. They talk us down where we are good, and attack some of the foundations of the trust and cooperation culture that are among our foremost competitive advantage. The Labour Party is opposed to these changes and will vote against them today, and we have also been aware that the proposal to open up temporaryity on a general basis is a change that will be of the most temporary nature. If we get a majority in 2017, we'll reverse this.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e1527	The government's proposals for changes to the Working Environment Act will create greater freedom in many workplaces. Recently, I visited a nursing home in Asker, Bondistanza, — housing for people with developmental disabilities. Both the manager and the employees were very pleased with the proposed decorations in the Working Environment Act. Almost all of the 50 employees want to work longer shifts on weekends to get twice as many weekends off. Only five–six employees have chosen to follow the usual rotation of work every three weekends. In this nursing home, they have applied for a derogation in order to be allowed to work double guards every six weeks instead of every three weekends. Because the tour is so flexible, many more employees are offered 100 pst. position. Sick leave is low, residents are satisfied. They do not have to deal with so many employees during the weekends, and they can add up to excursions and trips without having to take account of this break in the middle of the day with a shift change. With the government's new proposals, many more jobs themselves will be able to agree such schemes locally and not apply to central trade unions for law. The proposal opens up the possibility for a local health home to agree on a tour with watches of 12,5 hours and longer free periods. This means, for example: that they are able to cover the weekend by two shifts per day, as opposed to the day's provision, where a minimum of three different shifts are required on a daily basis. The changes in the rules on averaging could contribute to the reduction of overtime in periods of extra labour pressure. Norwegian work has changed a lot in the last ten years. Therefore, there is a need to make some adjustments to the Working Environment Act. Today we have a law it has become common to apply for an exception from. The government has proposed to make some moderate changes that are more adapted to today’s work and family life. These changes will provide a more flexible and modern working environment law. There will be no weakening of the law of such trade unions, with LO at the head, claims, but there will be a weakening of the power of the central trade unions, that is. a shift of power from central trade unions to local trustees and to the Labour Inspectorate. Marte Gerhardsen, head of the Agenda think tank, wrote in a chronicle in Today's Business that the veto court has expired. The left-hand side should listen a little bit more to her. She says, ‘Today, one of the parties is entitled to veto working time negotiations. It's basically problematic. The time is ripe for the government’s proposal to transfer the decision-making authority to the Labour Inspectorate.’;» The majority of lots will not weaken the Working Environment Act. On the other hand, we want to strengthen it and make it more adapted to today's work and family life. The Labour Environment Act is an important law that guarantees the rights of the worker in terms of health, environment and safety. The law will contribute to an inclusive working life, and it will have to be perceived as reasonable and minimise obstacles to good local solutions negotiated by workers and employers. Therefore, it is important and timely that central trade unions are deprived of the power they have to put down for solutions that both employees and employers want locally. Local trustees do not need ‘bodyguard’ from central trade unions; they are able to decide and decide for themselves, in close cooperation with those with whom they work. The Labour Inspectorate has the power to lower the foot if the contracts concluded are of health concern. Individuals and families live different lives. Workers and employers need flexibility and different solutions. Therefore, the government wants to open up alternative working time arrangements without having to apply for recognition from central trade unions. The government wants to be able to reach an agreement on this locally. Langturnus means that employees work longer and fewer shifts against corresponding more continuous leisure time. This gives more flexibility to the individual. The same applies to the average calculation of Sunday and public holidays. The Government proposes that the employer and the employee – as today – be able to agree on the average calculation of Sundays and holidays, so that the employee on average over 26 weeks will work half of the Sundays. There will be no more Sunday work, even though the LO and the left-hand side constantly falsify it. The change is that the rest of the week has to be spent on a Sunday and holidays at least every four weeks, towards every three weeks today. This means that students and others who have a lot of assignments and exams during periods can work fewer Sundays against working more consecutively in periods of more time. This proposal provides a flexibility that many workers actually regard as a good one. Today's practice of applying for a derogation for alternative working time arrangements is difficult and demanding. I know all about that after many years as a care and care manager. We have received input from several workplaces that would try alternative tours when there is an opportunity to arrange it locally. They did not have the power to initiate the exemption applications. In order to work in such a tour as Bondiranda – as I mentioned – a dispensation application must be made from the requirements of the Working Environment Act for central trade unions once a year. There is a lot of time to argue why you want to deviate from the requirements, and sometimes it is difficult to agree. The veto rights of the central trade unions will be lost by the new proposal. It involves more power and authority for local trustees, who, together with employees and employers, can agree locally, without interference from central trade unions.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e1669	Right has a long tradition of pushing weak groups and misunderstandings individual freedom over its arguments to weaken the protection of workers. Before the law on factory supervision was adopted in 1892, the party refused to regulate the working time of mature workers and explained that one should not prevent grown-up independent men from taking on as much work as they themselves would. Nor did the law prohibit child labour, as the labour movement demanded, and although it introduced a lower limit of 12 years, it did not follow with control mechanisms to ensure compliance with the laws. To ban child labour would be the same as taking the bread out of the mouth of many poor families, asserted Right then, and a ban on night work for young people between 14 and 18 would make it difficult for young people to get work. That's it. Now, today, it's the work environment law that's going to the press. The right party and the employers' organisations in my code are too good that it doesn't fit the reality of the day. So they're busy making work environment laws more flexible. This flexibility is just a tribute that hides the real agenda: reducing staff costs and making life easier for employers by shifting power from employees. The right hand side has always listened more to employers than to employees. The working party is critical to such a shift of power because the force ratio is already biased. The employer sits with the strongest cards, and only through strong laws and good professional organisation can this be smoothed out. If we weaken the work environment law, we give employers even greater control over the working day. If exceptions to the laws are to be made at the workplace, it is more difficult to say no to the individual worker. In fact, the work environment law is flexible and opens up too many local adaptations. Often it is the business leaders who complain the most about the law, who have at least looked into the scope of the law. If there is a local trade union, the employer may agree to a very large exemption from the main statutory working time rules. Large exceptions to working time arrangements are granted by the Labour Inspectorate and even greater exceptions are granted by national trade unions. These exceptional destinations are a kind of four-stage rocket that, as I see it, makes up an existing balance point. Your major exceptions to the main rules for working time, the higher your system needs to approve. We should note what kind of rhetoric the Right Party and the Progress Party uses on the Working Environment Act. They are systematic in their use of words about their own proposals for changes of promise: softening, flexibility, modern. In this way, the government will leave an impression of the work environment law that it is rigid, rigid, old-fashioned, immature. This is completely wrong. But this tactic is going to be the basis for the right-wing ideological changes that we're now seeing coming. The difference between the Labour Party and the Government is that we don't want to weaken the power of ordinary workers and give the organized working life poorer conditions. We don't want anyone else locked in the security of temporary additions. More evening and weekend jobs, longer days and more temporary additions are not emulations; there are restrictions on employees. We don't want a work environment law that only fits the healthy and fast ones and those who can do their work with the iPad from the sofa hook in the evening. A safe and decent working life is an important part of the Norwegian social model, which means that we are at the top level internationally when it comes to combining even income distribution, good welfare, competitiveness and innovation. It is precisely this government that is at stake when they claim to modernise their working life and soften their work environment laws. On the other hand, the changes they propose will lead to a harder work life, and their historical failure is to believe that a more modern work life will be restored.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e2566	There has been a radical change in working life in recent decades. Those of us who have good education, regular work and need good Norwegian knowledge as a working language have had an incredible real income increase – about 50 pst. in the last 15 years. It's almost unreal. We at Stortinget, employees or representatives, are serving on these developments in the world of work. We serve on the combination of serving well and having good pay and working conditions and having access to cheap services — services made by people on low wages abroad, or produced in Norway by people working for far lower income than tariffs. There is an accelerating trend with major differences in labour income. When, in addition, we have increasing differences in job-free income between different groups, there are reportedly different wealth distributions, there are differences in the overall economic situation in which people are. This breaks with the agility, the agility that is Norway’s historic European advantage. It's incredibly important to be aware of what's happening. The EEA Agreement has worked for more than 20 years, with free movement of labour within the EU/EEA countries. From 2004, with the east enlargement, we saw a radical increase in labour migration. Per Rune Henriksen of the Workers' Party described the developments in his trade, the timber trade, and the situation of his former colleagues in a good way. In our general remarks, we in the Center Party describe the strength of Norwegian employment. We describe the strength of high levels of employment, high average working hours per person of working age — important prerequisites for financing universal public rights in the future. In the reporting part of this proposition, according to the opinion of the Centre Party, it is under-communicated that, as a result of increased labour migration, we are now in the midst of a trend of declining employment rates and lower average working hours per person of working age. The centre party believes that it only makes things worse if we try to solve future challenges related to the financing of our common welfare systems of labour immigration, as a result of the very falling employment of the increased labour market in the population. Instead of investing in labour immigration to solve our problems, the Centre Party believes that we need to get even more of our citizens to work. People must be grown and educated, while at the same time facilitating participation of their citizens in Norwegian employment under Norwegian wage and work conditions. This is a formidable political task, which the Central Party will give very high priority to. As many people here today have described from experience, EEA immigration challenges wage formation in Norway negatively, especially in professions where one does not need to master Norwegian as a working language. The Central Party believes that, in order to avoid this, the Storting must adopt legislation regulating the supply of foreign labour, and we therefore propose that the Stortinget ask the Government to work for regulations aimed at delimiting labour immigration from the EEA territory. Weak groups in the labour market today are losing when access to cheap, young and diligent labour – and I cannot get enough of this with hard labour – that comes through labour immigration, is as large as it is today. We have to talk about this loudly. It should have been done a long time ago by multiple parties. The centre party believes that we need far stronger instruments to increase demand for those groups of people who lose their jobs. Here, the payroll schemes are important. I hope that the vague wording that comes in the message part will be given a completely different content, so that there may be something out of it. Otherwise, in the case of the permanent work scheme, the so-called VTA scheme, it is important that it remains part-financed by the State and is a state scheme. It's very central, and a very dear arrangement for the many who have enjoyed it. There is a galloping growth in manning companies, and an increasing number of people who have the form of contract permanent employment without pay between jobs. It now shows that the Storting must take into account the definition of ‘permanent employment’ and ‘temporary employment’, and that one must also make the form of a contract of fixed employment without remuneration between assignments illegal. It's very important that we do that, because we can't have the obscenities that evolve now, that people get the impression that it's a fixed employment, and then reality is something completely different. It should have been unnecessary for us to define fixed-term employment, but it is now necessary.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e3414	Let there be no doubt: the government's clear message is that fixed employment should be the general rule of employment. ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e3435	I am a little surprised at where some representatives from the Right pick up the knowledge from. The figures are quite clear that temporary employment acts as a springboard into working life if the rules around it are strict and you do not allow temporaryity as a general rule – as in Norway, where you do if it is actually a temporary situation. This is one of the latest research reports that have studied how this works with different laws in very similar labour markets. Unfortunately, the government's party is wrong.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e3649	At the request of the Labour and Social Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to the government. ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e3658	I would first like to take the opportunity to thank the members of the Committee for a quick and constructive feedback along the way in their work. Although not everyone agrees, it is my opinion that the members of the Committee have a fundamental respect for each other's views. Prop. 55 S concerns minimum requirements for strengthening worker mobility between Member States by improving performance and continuing the right to supplementary pensions. The aim of the Directive is to facilitate the exercise by workers of the right to freedom of movement and to contribute to the mobility of workers by reducing the obstacles arising from certain rules on supplementary pension entitlements linked to employment. In the field of pensions, the social security of workers is ensured through statutory social security schemes together with supplementary occupational pension schemes, which are increasingly common in European countries. In many ways, it is natural to view Europe as a single labour market where people in different countries find work across national borders. When unemployment increases in one European country, many people choose to move to another to get a job. This gives many families a better life. In recent years, European countries have had close cooperation in the university and higher education sectors to coordinate and develop common understanding of academic degrees and character systems. This makes it easier to seek work in other European countries because two training courses can be compared. A good example is Norwegian students studying medicine in Poland, and then they return to Norway to practice. The EEA is important for Norway, and our relationship with Europe is about jobs in Norway, but also about access to the European market for business. It is important to ensure that workers are able to seek work in other European countries, therefore the obstacles to the right to free movement and mobility of workers should be reduced. It is important to stress that the Directive applies only to those workers who switch jobs across borders and not to those who change jobs within the same Member State. The Directive will have little impact on existing national legislation and rules on supplementary pension schemes, since the Norwegian rules are essentially in line with the rules of the Directive.

On the day the farming industry does not grow until it has solved its environmental problems, the farming industry solves the environmental problems. Over the last decades, Norwegian farming industry has over and over again assured that only in a short time will it have solved its environmental problems, and that there is no danger to wild species, marine trout or local fauna. It's now a thousand times more farmed salmon than wild salmon along the Norwegian coast. Leak resistance, leaching agents release, plant waste, escape, non-reporting and non-tracking potential continue to damage wild salmon, sea trout, crustaceans and ecosystems. In addition, the problems of fishing welfare are increasing. So even though lice and escape as a percentage of the farming industry are falling, the growth is making sure that the pressures on the natural environment of the coast remain unsustainable. Storing and governments have repeatedly announced, and adopted, zero visions, action plans, measures and regulations, but they have refused to implement the kind of measures and clear demands and responses that we know work. The result is so bad that various fisheries ministers should blush. The size knows well how to clean up industries. As the representative of Fylkesnes just pointed out, from the late 1970s Norway carried out a great cleaning process in the Norwegian industry. Emissions of all types of pollution decreased dramatically and the mechanism was clear requirements, cash enforcement and unsentimental responses. The result was brilliant and to the delight of absolutely all. That is why the Green Party is proposing to use the environmentally logical instrument in the farming industry, which speaks the language that the farming industry will understand the best: halt the growth of environmental problems has been solved! I encourage the Committee to test whether these indicators meet its objectives by applying them to the critical loop situation that we had in the summer of 2014. If the proposed system of the Storing Communication is to have any hope of working for the future, the state of the environment in 2014 should result in blood-red lights along large parts of the coast and extensive slaughtering to reduce the lice pressure. So once again, when the Minister of Fisheries is falsifying about fivefold Norwegian farming, I hope she and the Committee will also lend ear to the world's largest and most professional breeder, Marine Harvest, who is saying – and I repeat: the industry must take the challenges it faces, seriously before the big growth can come. This is about further developing Norway’s second largest export industry in an environmentally sustainable way. ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4005	The representative of Blattmann has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4008	Catch of shark products is one of the most grotesque ways of taxing an animal. More precisely, you only tax parts of an animal. Although the practice of throwing the rest of the animal alive at sea is largely prohibited, the capture of sharks for fins has wiped out a number of sharks and has caused almost 100 sharks to be endangered. The most common practice for catching shark products is ‘hairfinning’. Most sharks are caught using longlines. This is done by laying a line of everything from one kilometer to 100 kilometers, equipped with up to 16,000 hooks, out into the sea and taken up the following morning. The catch is very varied and sometimes very brutal. Everything from dolphins, seals, seabirds, small fish and sharks gets caught at hooks. The placing on the market of sharks will pose a threat to shark populations even if the catch does not involve sharking. About 100 million sharks are caught every year. It is significantly more than what is considered to be the sustainable taxation of sharks. The numbers are uncertain, but scientists claim that the total number can be as high as 273 million sharks killed per year. It's about 20 pst. of the total shark population. By eradicating the top part of the food chain, enormous challenges are being created downwards. You create a bias in the ecosystem. The values are huge. Since 2000, the total import value of sharks has been between 2.4 billion and 3.5 billion. kr. Although Norway is not, of course, a problem with the sharks themselves, there is no excuse for leaning back and believing that we have completely clean hands. The signal effect of Norway taking this challenge seriously is likely to be more effective than we might think. Although a ban on sharking is in place, the high value of sharks will still make it attractive to try to circumvent the rules and practice sharking. Therefore, there is a need for stricter regulation of imports of products from sharks in order to stop imports of illegal shark products into the Norwegian market. The Christian People's Party, together with the Greens, therefore believes that it is necessary to introduce the requirement that shark products imported must have full species information, which will ensure that threatened shark species do not reach the Norwegian market. The products imported must also have complete and verifiable information on the method, location, date and time of capture. The position of the rapporteur points to the CITES list of endangered sharks. It is worth noting that only eight of nearly 100 endangered sharks are included in the CITES list. The remaining shark species are largely lacking protection against over-taxation, and the Christian People's Party and the Greens therefore believe that CITES does not provide a good enough level of protection. It also happens that sharks, both threatened and unfeared, are fished in areas that are actually protected areas where sharks are not to be taken. This may include, inter alia: occur in cases where the shark is by-catch in relation to other fishing activities, such as: fishing for tuna by means of non-authorised fishing methods. There are also some areas protected during given periods of the year. That is why we believe that there is a need for both the timing and the location and the method of fishing to be clearly identified in relation to imports. By making demands in line with this proposal, Norway will be a driver for reducing illegal, unregulated and unreported fishing. I bring up the proposals of the Christian People's Party. The Central Party and the Workers' Party have a proposal in the vote, promoted by the Workers' Party now, and the Christian People's Party will vote for it as a secondary vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4081	The world population of animal species is on average reduced to about half of what they were just 40 years ago. Species are eradicated at between a hundred and a thousand times the rate now that has been natural in the last hundred million years. That means we're on a very steep outcurrency in eradicating a lot of species. It is above all the large animals that are at risk – large mammals, large birds, large crustaceans, large shellfish and large fishes. These species together make up the ecosystems that we depend on, and the major species have key roles in their ecosystems. Besides, the big animals are a pretty important part of our image by nature. They are important because we think it is beautiful, exciting and valuable in nature. A nature without large animals is in many ways a poor nature. You can imagine a sea without sharks, it's a poor and unexpecting sea. Of course, all the representatives here agree that we need to stop this species eradication, and most people will probably agree that we also need to take new steps to do so in practice. This case is a test of the will to do this in practice. Because it is the overall impact of many small individual decisions that eliminate species, directly or indirectly, and it is also the overall impact of many small individual decisions that may allow them to be saved. Of course, the matter we are dealing with here may seem curious to many. Taking decisions on the labelling of shark products is, of course, a rather narrow exercise, but the situation is that in this case Norway can actively choose to go beyond international legislation and conventional methods, thus setting a new standard that attracts global development. If you can do that, it's worth taking a little slave with you to get it done. The CITES Convention, to which many have referred, shall monitor international trade in endangered species of animals and plants. It shall verify exactly what the shark products are about, which is a major threat to these extremely old and extremely specialised species, which are definitely going to be eradicated if trade does not come under control. So, as the committee points out, as many people have pointed out, CITES only has eight of the nearly hundreds of endangered shark species on its list. This is due to formal and political opposition from countries, including Norway, which prioritises various principal and more or less real economic interests rather than halting the eradication of sharks. That is why the Greens are very pleased – as it now looks, and if we have not misunderstood anything – that we can now at least have a majority for one of the proposals, to ask the government to move on to this. We are pleased that, at least, it is recognised that there is a problem when, as some committee members say with reference to the Minister for Fisheries, listing eight hundred species constitutes a so-called good level of protection. It's not this kind of inconclusive position that's going to bring the work to rescue endangered species in the future. So we're facing an escalating eradication problem, where we have to do something new to solve it. The shark market exists because it's lucrative, and because it's badly controlled, and making it better controlled and less lucrative is exactly what will work. Therefore, it is very necessary that Norway is also willing to try something new. The Greens will therefore, of course, vote in favour of our own proposals and also of the proposal promoted by the Central Party and the Labour Party, which I understand will be supported by the Left and which I hope will be supported by more parties.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4099	In my reply letter of 16 December 2014, I have explained Norwegian administrative policy and the reasons I believe are important in terms of overfishing and illegal fishing of living resources in the sea. Imports into Norway of sharks are very modest and represented a value of only about about in 2013. NOK 13 000. These imports originate mainly from Denmark, Spain and New Zealand, all of which have ratified the CITES Convention and are considered to have good administrative regimes. According to the FAO, the catch of sharks has fallen in the last decade and is currently at about 760,000 tonnes. The main weight of shark fishing is in tropical waters, the Indian Ocean and the Pacific Ocean. Total catches of fish have been around 90 million tonnes per year in recent years. The minority of the Committee gives the impression that Norway does not appear to be concerned with shark management or does not take seriously the loss of biodiversity. The UN General Assembly’s Fisheries Resolution shows that Norway and many other countries are also concerned with the management and taxation of sharks and with specific issues related to shark fishing. The resolution points to a number of measures that should be taken by the countries catching sharks and when such products are placed on the market — a turnover that takes place primarily in some Asian countries. The International Shark Management Action Plan was adopted in 1999 and the FAO reports on both progress and challenges in the area of sound regulation of sharks. It is known that the regional fisheries organisations or the coastal and flag States concerned are responsible for managing resources in a sustainable manner. I would also like to stress that, in terms of the management of shark populations in our waters, we take sound management measures and regulate fishing based on the actual biological status of the individual shark populations. In my written reply I have explained why I believe the vote proposal 2 is not feasible because in many cases the requested information will not be available. There will also be very high resource requirements for Customs, which will be the department responsible for verifying and verifying such documentation. I do not see that the minority of the Economic and Social Committee in its justification for the proposal has assessed how reprobability can be achieved when the necessary information will not be obtainable. I also note that this type of requirement will be much more stringent than the requirements generally imposed on imports of fish and fishery products, where the challenges of over-taxation and over-fishing are a key challenge for sustainable management of marine resources. In this respect, Norway is also bound by our international commitments, including in the WTO, that trade measures should not be more radical than necessary. I would like to stress that the means of combating over-taxation and illegal fishing must be based on the same principles, while at the same time there must be consistency between the nature of the problem and the means of achieving this objective. Norway has been the leader in the fight against over-taxation and illegal fishing. In this work, we have put the crucial emphasis on international solutions and instruments for how to find good solutions. The same requirement must also apply with regard to sharks. Also allow me to use this opportunity to tell Stortinget that today we have established North Atlantic Fisheries Intelligence Networks which are just a cooperation between seven countries and the OECD to combat illegal, unregulated and unreported fishing globally. Here is Norway in the driver’s seat, where fishing authorities, tax authorities and customs authorities are directly involved in combating, detecting and ensuring that illegal fishing is sanctioned. But I am concerned that the measures and resources we are going to put in must be proportionate to the results that can be achieved.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4598	The Green Party places personal responsibility high, whether it is personal responsibility each of us has for the future of the earth, or it is personal responsibility we have to take on the consequences of the things we choose to do in everyday life and leisure. In Norway, as a rule, people take responsibility for their own security in activities that we carry out voluntarily. We go for a walk in the woods with sharp weapons, we dive to 30 meters deep in the night, we go skiing alone in the middle of the winter, and some jump out of high stuff with nice dresses on them. The state does not oblige us to use safety equipment when we do these things. It just asks us to use our heads, and otherwise it wants us good luck. Moving in nature on nature's premises and on its own responsibility is a good tradition, and we're going to keep it. But about 2 million Norwegians are on a boat trip in a year. We have about 850,000 recreational boats, and we're moving more and more on the sea. So the question of a life jacket directive concerns a lot of people. Fortunately, since 1970, the number of deaths at sea has been halved. Much of that improvement is attributed to attitude-creating work. But in 2014, 34 people drowned from recreational boats, and 27 of them drowned because they didn't have a vest. 34 deaths are 34 too many. The probability of getting killed if you land in the sea without floating equipment is 12 times greater than if you have floating equipment. Life jackets obviously make it easier to find people when the accident occurs. The cost of society is, as you know, great when it comes to looking for people who have been killed without a life jacket. Above all, the load comes on relatives, who lose someone they might not have lost if they had used a life jacket. The likelihood of fewer drowning accidents and the tragedies they bring are heavy arguments for the directive on lifejackets. Moreover, much experience suggests that it has an eye-catching effect in addition to the direct effect of the directive. At the same time, there is no doubt that a general directive on the use of lifejackets for everyone in a boat less than eight metres on board can become a comprehensive directive and be seen as a rather rigid intervention. In blank summer days, there are probably several people who want to make up their own mind about how literally they want to take such a mandate, and the father is of course present for people to practice a full mandate relatively much at their own discretion. That is why, after previous treatment, the government has come back to Stortinget with a proposal to place a life jacket only for children under 16. We see this as an honest attempt to soften a rigid directive, but the problem with the proposal is that this age group is already the best person to use a life jacket. The most vulnerable group for drowning accidents is known as men over 40 years of age. In other words, a ban for children up to 16 years of age is completely in place on those groups that are most vulnerable. Safety at sea is primarily – and will always be – a matter of knowledge, responsibility and judgment. Safety equipment will help, but it will never replace personal responsibility and knowledge. That is why the Greens share their views on rescue measures in the Environment Party. But taking responsibility itself also means taking responsibility for listening to people and listening to professionals. We know that the working group, the rescue company and the police have fully assessed the injunctions and the objections to full injunctions, and they are very clear that a warrant will work and can be implemented. Besides, people want a warrant. This takes the Green Party's responsibility for taking this seriously. We therefore support the proposal to amend the Kleinbootsgesetz § 23 so that a full directive on the use of life jackets will apply.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4655	Norway is a boat nation. A 2012 survey shows that almost half of the Norwegian population are on board a boat during the season. Therefore, it is natural that the Storting is engaged in recreational boating – and not least safety related to this. This political attention is also reflected in the strict requirements and rules for the use of recreational craft in Norway. The government is now proposing a further tightening of the law, as a result of the Storting decision last year. We have therefore put forward a proposal on the imposition of floating equipment for children and young people under 16. The government's proposal is based on a balance of several factors. I think the authorities should be careful about regulating people's leisure activities and natural experiences. We must have respect for the freedom that natural experiences and recreation give us. I believe in freedom under responsibility, and sometimes there should be limits to politics. It also acknowledges that it is not all society can and should regulate itself out of it. A number of activities would pose risks, including: in the wild, both by land and by sea. Today we have a directive to have floating equipment available to everyone on board a recreational boat. This requirement well balances the need for good safety on board a recreational boat as a society, while retaining responsibility for use on an individual basis. With the government's proposals, I believe we have found an appropriate balance. Children and young people under the age of 16 may not always be expected to maintain their own safety in the same way as adults. They are also more vulnerable in an accident situation. In the survey I referred to initially, it turns out that as many as 80 pst. they use floating equipment “always” or “always” when in a recreational boat. Although boating can always lead to risk, it is not necessarily such a risk that it requires state regulation. We know that boating in Norway is very diverse. This means that general instructions also affect those who already show good seafarership, or those who judge for themselves that they do not need to use floating equipment. The most important thing to prevent drowning accidents is that people have knowledge of safety at sea. The Maritime Directorate is therefore continuously working on awareness-raising work. So does the Rescue Company. That being said, of course, any death as a result of drowning is too much. But we know that mind-creating work brings results. It is not least important for young boat users. A requirement for the youngest will also be educational and educational, so that they hopefully continue to use flotation equipment as adults. Therefore, I believe that the government's proposals will increase the attention on the safety and use of floating equipment, while respecting the freedom of adults to decide for themselves.
