ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4887	From one freedom debate to another, you might say. In February Palestine’s friends at Stortinget met here with representatives from the Military Court Watch, who are monitoring the situation, especially for children in Israeli prisons. They were very concerned that there had to be a stronger international pressure on Israel to make progress so that these children could have their fundamental rights fulfilled. On March 17 there was a new election in Israel. It was not a good day for all of us who hope and believe that a two-state solution is still possible. Netanyahu won the election to frighten that the Arabs flocked to the polls, and by promising that a Palestinian state would never see the light of day with him by power. Unfortunately, there have been few points of light in the peace process, and for many Palestinians the situation has gone from bad to worse in recent years. I myself have been in Palestine regularly in the last twelve years, and there are many strong impressions, especially the impression of meeting individuals. I visited a warm summer day one of the few Palestinian families who still live in their own house in the illegal settlement of Beit Hadassah in the old town of Hebron. One daughter in the family had just been subjected to violence by the settlers who surrounded their house. She came with her arm in the barrel and had sewn many stitches in her head. The man who had committed the assault was never punished. No investigation was even opened. Violence against Palestinians from settlers is often reported, but two-thirds of the cases are committed without any legal follow-up. Later in the day, we were at the base of the old town. Then we saw how stone-throwing Palestinian children were being punished. There came a whole bunch of soldiers marching through narrow streets. In the middle, there were three boys -- they couldn't have been more than 12-13 years. All the boys had a gun pointing at the back of their heads. That's how they were pushed through the old town on their way to the police station to set an example. And while I was being brutally arrested for throwing stones, I thought of the settler who had subjected a little girl to gross violence, and who was allowed to walk around quite freely in the same city. This unfair discrimination creates a breeding ground for recklessness and desperation, and it is in this pit that extremism causes fertiliser to grow. The three boys I was witnessing were arrested in Hebron, not a single case. This was just one of many examples of systematic imprisonment of Palestinian children. Each year between 500 and 700 children are arrested and prosecuted in Israeli military courts. There is no other country, according to UNICEF, where children are systematically placed on military youth courts. By definition, these courts cannot guarantee that children’s rights are respected. Israel is also repeatedly criticized for violations of the Children's Convention. In February 2013, UNICEF launched a report which concluded that child rights violations are comprehensive, systematic and institutionalised. Often the children are not informed of their rights. They often do not have access to water, toilet or medical assistance. Many are subjected to physical and mental violence and are often pressured to sign a confession written in a language they do not understand. UNICEF therefore recommended 38 points that had to be fulfilled in order for Israel to comply with the Children’s Convention. But when the UN Human Rights Committee last November dealt with the human rights situation in Israel, they once again said that the Committee recognises that there has been a positive development on paper, but they are concerned that the reforms do not lead to a change in practice and repeated again the criticism. In February of this year, UNICEF presented a new report which reviews the 38 recommendations they made two years ago. There are some flashes of light. There are small steps in the right direction, but many of the measures still will not benefit children because they are often suspected of security breaches and are not covered by these good measures. For example, Military Order 1745 says that all child questioning is to be video-monitored, but this does not apply where matters relate to safety, such as safety. for children throwing stones. The UNICEF report also shows that the abuse of Palestinian children continues. During the period 2013 to September 2014, UNICEF went through 208 cases involving Palestinian children in Israeli prisons. 171 of those children said that they were subjected to physical violence, and 163 of the children said that they had not been informed of their rights – especially important is the right of a lawyer or the right not to speak. We still have a long way to go before the Palestinian children get their fundamental rights fulfilled. There is reason to commend both UNICEF, Military Court Watch, lawyers and civil society for the thorough work done to document what is happening, and to exert pressure on Israel. I also want to thank all the Norwegian organisations that have recently run a campaign on this at home. They have spread knowledge and commitment to the rights of these children. This debate is about the fact that all people have rights that cannot be broken and that children have a special right to protection. But it is also about how we can contribute to a peaceful solution to the conflict. Investing in children and young people is investing in the future. It's these kids who are going to grow up and live side by side with their Israeli neighbors. It's these kids that I hope someday can realize a two-state solution. It is said that a good childhood lasts life, but too many of these children have no good childhood to build on. In Gaza, children who are now ten years old have already experienced three wars – three wars where children and civilians are the most badly hit, wars where it is not possible to escape from the bombs, because Gaza de facto is the world's largest prison. Everyone has somehow lost someone in the family or someone they know, and when the war was over, schools and homes were buried, and the same could probably also be said about the hope for many children. On the West Bank, children grow up and see their country getting smaller and smaller, their homes are being demolished by Israeli authorities to make room for more settlements, so hundreds of children are being imprisoned and traumatized every year. Emad Al-Rozzi has researched refugee children and traumas. He visited last year's Globalisation Conference in Oslo. He told about the consequences for many of the children who were arrested. ‘The children become depressed. They lose interest in playing, communicating and socializing. They draw away from others, from family and friends. They have low communication and concentration capabilities. [[...]] They also lose interest in laughing." This does not create a ground for a peaceful solution to an excessive conflict. I therefore look forward to the Foreign Minister explaining – at least to the question of – how he will help to ensure that children also in Israeli prisons meet their rights to legal certainty and human rights.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e4926	Let me first thank the representative Håheim for an important and timely interplay. The government shares the concern of arrested Palestinian children. This is a topic the Ministry of Foreign Affairs priorities to address both in bilateral talks and in the relevant UN bodies. Already during my first visit to Israel in November 2013, I addressed this issue with Israeli authorities. By this month, Secretary of State Bård Glad Pedersen has also raised the question in his conversations in Israel. I also want to refer to Report. St. l0 for 2014–2015, Possibilities for all – human rights as objectives and means of foreign and development policy. This Communication is for the consideration of the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence Policy. The government here determines that it is a priority area to work to protect children in armed conflict and to combat violence against children. Children and young people should not be deprived of their freedom. Prison without legal law and judgment is unacceptable. When children and young people are imprisoned without law and judgment, this is a double wrong. Nevertheless, in line with relevant international conventions in this area, the human rights and legal certainty of children must be respected. UNICEF, Military Court Watch, to which the interpellant refers, as well as Israeli human rights organisations, have all done a detailed and important job of documenting violations and violations of fundamental principles against imprisoned Palestinian children. Too many Palestinian children are arrested every year. There is also evidence that too many of these children are exposed to violence and other abuses that no child should experience. UNICEF has put forward 38 proposals to reform the Israeli military prison system to ensure that children’s rights are respected. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has over time reminded Israeli authorities that the treatment to which children are exposed violates international rules that Israel has committed itself to comply with. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has stressed that children are a particularly vulnerable group and that their rights must always be respected. Civil society is important in order to draw attention to the rights of children. Norway supports Israeli human rights organisations working for the rights of Palestinian children. We have also supported a rehabilitation project directed by Redd Barna and Norwegian KFUM and YMCA in East Jerusalem for Palestinian children who have been in Israeli prisons. The Israeli Ministry of Justice points out on its side that 70–75 pst. of all offences committed by minors on the West Bank who are being prosecuted by Israel, involve the use of violence. This could be throwing rocks or molotov cocktails. From the Military Court, we have been informed that although many younger children are also arrested by Israeli soldiers, no Palestinian children under the age of 12 and no children between the ages of 12 and 14 have been imprisoned in 2014. Seven out of eight Palestinian minors in prison are between 16 and 18. In talks with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Israeli authorities stress that they attach great importance to the rights of children. They state that there have been several important changes in recent years, and that the Israeli State Attorney has instructed the Israeli defence to exercise the utmost care in the face of minor attacks on the West Bank. UNICEF also confirms in its reports – as the interpellant had in mind – that there have been several improvements in relevant Israeli legislation and instructions. Specific measures include raising the age of authority of Palestinian children to 18 in 2011, in line with the Convention on the Rights of the Child. A shorter limitation period for offences committed by minors has also been introduced. Furthermore, the prison period for the first trial is limited, and measures are in place to improve the warning system against offenders and the information provided to minors about their rights. Military officers have also been instructed to avoid arrests of children at night, which Norway has also been concerned about. According to organisations, the number of children from the West Bank in Israeli prisons is halved from 2009 to 2014. Here it turns out that international pressure and pressure in the region is working. Intensification of Israeli legislation — and circular — shows that international attention to fundamental human rights is important and results. At the same time, it appears, unfortunately, that changes to the legislation do not necessarily result in sufficient changes to the ground. Too many Palestinian minors are still being imprisoned. Organisations also report from their studies that a majority of minors respond to the fact that they have both been subjected to violence and have not been met by either the deputy or the lawyer for questioning. This is not acceptable. We expect Israel's own goals to actually be pursued. Those responsible at political level must be assured that they are making changes on the ground. Children need special protection. Therefore, they have expressed their own rights in the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Children, which is 25 years old this year. The Children's Convention gives children the same basic rights, no matter who they are, and where they live. Norway is working to strengthen the implementation of the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child in International Fora, and in particular in the United Nations. This work also focuses on the legal certainty of children. Among other things, we have actively supported the decision of the United Nations General Assembly of December last year asking the Secretary-General of the United Nations to conduct a study and monitoring of the conditions of children deprived of freedom. This study is to be started. Furthermore, Norway provides support to the Secretary-General’s Special Representative for Violence against Children and the Secretary-General’s Special Representative for Children and Armed Conflict, as well as to non-governmental organisations both in Israel and in Norway working to strengthen the legal security of children. The government will continue to address the situation of children with Israel, and we will continue our cooperation with like-minded countries to promote respect for the international conventions in this area.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e5001	The interpellant has raised an important issue, for the fact that children are charged and sentenced in military courts is totally unacceptable. The UN Convention on the Rights of the Child stresses that all children have fundamental rights related to survival, participation, development and protection against discrimination. Article 37 of the Convention on the Rights of the Child states that children shall not be subjected to torture or violence. Norway must continue to work for the protection of children in armed conflicts and the fight against violence against children and, as the Minister for Foreign Affairs said, Norway is actively working to strengthen the Children’s Convention. The Children’s Convention has been ratified by almost all countries in the world, but a number of countries have made extensive reservations. In recent years, there have been positive developments in key areas such as education and survival. At the same time, public authorities need to prioritise resources for fulfilling the rights of children to life, development, health and education. The message is imperative and timely. Fundamental human rights are under increasing pressure internationally. Participation of children and young people is a good investment in future active citizens who can assert their rights socially, economically and politically. The joy was therefore great as one could read in the newspapers last summer that the council would now act and send to Stortinget a bill that strengthened the legal protection of the mob victims. For example: a notice from the NTB on 11 June read something the Council of State said: ‘In the government platform, we establish that it is the bully who has to switch schools to bullying cases where school exchanges are the only solution. There have been some uncertainties and different practices in relation to whether and how the bully can be moved in such cases’. So here the law and the law were to be tightened up. The bullies were supposed to be stronger. The surprise was great when we got a bill proposal with two changes in the laws, where it is quite specific to both changes in the law that these changes do not change the legal state. We have tried to see if it has ever been sent a bill from a government where the entire bill does not change the legal status, and we have good reason to find it. We have found examples that in connection with other legal work one also clears up linguistic ambiguity, perhaps the order of paragraphs and such things, but that an entire bill is sent to the Stortinget without changing the law, is a new Norwegian record. It is said from the state council that this is because it has been unclear whether it has been possible to move on the bully instead of the bullying victim. On questions about what enquiries the Council of State has received about this during the whole of 2014, we were told that it was a matter of a handful of enquiries. We have been sent the enquiries, and it turns out that there are no schools or principals that have at all been in doubt that the law was ready. However, an overall committee will support these changes in order not to raise the opposite doubt, namely that we should be against the fact that the legal status should continue to be as it was before the Council of State sent a legislative amendment to the Stortingen, so that we do not create any new confusion. But I must say very seriously that bullying is an area that is better suited for action and action than political marketing without content. It is okay to stand in the VG and say that now you should lock the door at the school for those who are late, even though schools have practised it long before the Council of State became the Council of State. It is okay to stand in the VG and say that the schools should be given the opportunity to confiscate mobile phones and computer equipment, if it is misused, even though most schools have had this in their legislation long before the Council of State became the Council of State. It's okay to say that you have to take 4 in math to get into the teacher's education, even though you're actually reducing the requirement to 2, given that you're taking a math course to get in. In a sense, there are not questions that go to the core of school security. However, making difficult headlines to change the law and strengthen the legal certainty of the bullying victims, who are plagued every day, and then making a legislative amendment that does not strengthen the legal certainty of the bullying victim 1 mm, is leading the weakest in Norway behind the light. I'm upset by it. I think it's a shame to use such an area to do nothing. It is possible to rectify this by voting in favour of the proposal to return to work that strengthens the position of the victims of bullying. It'll show up if we can get a majority for that. I take up the proposal herein.

Today we have fit seven billion people, but only seven pst. are more European. This is not a circuit championship, Norway championship or European championship in setting bold goals; it is a global virtue that must be measured not in good intentions but in real results. We must succeed in Paris until the fall. The tests have been numerous. Unfortunately, the disappointments have been the same. Nor in Paris is there reason to believe that there will be an agreement that will correct the fundamental problem: that it is too cheap to pollute and too expensive to be environmentally friendly. Fortunately, more and more countries, regions and state agencies are introducing different forms of emission pricing. Contamination will be more expensive. At the same time, we are on the threshold of a paradigm shift in the power sector and in the transport sector. The prices of the sun and the wind are dramatically reduced in a short time. The prices of batteries for energy storage for power generation and electric mobility have fallen faster than anyone could predict. The investment universe is being expanded. In the United States, solar energy is now competitive with fossil alternatives in 36 countries. In South Africa, US$14 billion has been invested in solar and wind power plants in the last three and a half years alone, with a total of 4 000 MW. The green shift is in progress. We're on the threshold of a new era. Norway has technology, competence and capital to lead. There is broad consensus in the Committee that Norway must continue to give priority to work on technology development in industry. Hydro’s new pilot plant at Karmøy, which will become the world’s cleanest aluminium production plant, is a good example of what a good tool can do to trigger. The government has strengthened both Enova's Climate Technology Fund and Innovation Norway's Environmental Technology Scheme. Overall, Norway is well equipped to contribute to the development of new environmentally friendly technologies, which can both help reduce emissions and at the same time strengthen Norway’s competitiveness under green conditions. The petroleum industry in Norway will be important for a long time in the future. The world’s highest carbon prices provide good incentives to develop new solutions that reduce emissions and streamline production. At the same time, the high-level environment in the offshore industry has significant transfer value to other industries in the green shift. The government rates significantly on renewable energy production, not least through an increase in State-owned equity of 10 billion. kr and better framework conditions for wind power and small-scale power. In total, this provides the basis for more green jobs. Norway also shows roads in the transport sector. So far this year is 18 pst. of new car sales of zero-emission cars. In no other country, this proportion is close to being the same. We show that it's possible. The emission reductions from this investment are currently limited in Norway, but the contribution we make to the development of the industry and through the power of the example show that zero-emission mobility is both possible and feasible should not be underestimated. The technological paradigm shift may come faster than anyone had foreseen. At the same time, it will take time to phase it in globally. Still, there are 1.3 billion people who have no access to electricity at all. Still, it's almost 40 pst. Of the world's population who depend on cooking over open fire, not because they want to, but because there are no other alternatives. There is broad consensus in the Committee that Norway must continue its international commitment to reduce emissions in developing countries and contribute to sustainable development. The government has promised $1.6 billion. kr to the UN Green Fund for the next four years. The international forestry effort shall be continued until 2020. Originally, it was planned to last five years. The results are good and annual reductions in deforestation contribute to emission reductions corresponding to the total emissions of Norway. If we take the international forest investment into account, we're already carbon neutral today. The emphasis on renewable energy in developing countries is also of great importance. SN Power and Norfund are building renewable energy for billions in developing countries. This work also triggers significant private investment. We see, for example: that privacy Scatec Solar implements a number of projects in cooperation with Norfund. The need for energy will increase dramatically in the years to come as a result of a global emerging middle class. Unfortunately, we see that coal still dominates in very many economies. In Poland, coal-fired power stations emit 37 million tonnes of CO2 every year. All of the 10 largest emission sources in Europe are coal-fired power plants, which together emit more than four times the total emissions of Norway. For political and economic reasons, coal will remain important in many countries. It is therefore important that we succeed in developing a cost-effective technology for the clean-up and storage of CO2. Norway is working here widely to contribute to this work, and it must continue. The market is a miserable master, but it's the most efficient server we have. When you think about it green, you start a snowball that doesn't stop. At the same time, we can influence how fast the snowball is going to roll. The way we target our climate goals is important to our ability to achieve the goals we set ourselves. The Government's proposal for the 2030 climate targets is broadly endorsed by the Committee. This is very gratifying, because today's decisions set the stage for social development in Norway for a long time to come. The Government has proposed a commitment to be submitted to the United Nations Convention on Climate Change with the following elements: firstly, Norway will assume a contingent obligation of at least 40 pst. emissions reduction in 2030 compared to 1990; secondly, Norway will engage in dialogue with the EU to enter into a joint agreement to meet climate commitments with the EU with a climate target of at least 40 pst. in 2030 compared to 1990 levels. This will provide a predictable and binding framework for further development of Norwegian climate policy. Norway is already closely integrated into the EU internal market in Europe through the EEA Agreement. 80 pst. Norway’s exports go to the EU. In the interests of Norwegian industry and industry, it is therefore reasonable that Norway should also be more closely associated with the EU in climate policy. An agreement with the EU means that there is a stronger distinction between the quota and non-quota sectors. Emissions in the ETS sector in the EEA will be reduced by 43 pst. in 2030 compared to 2005. The quota market will gradually be tightened up to ensure this. Norwegian enterprises in the quota market will have both flexibility and incentives to further develop a strong green industrial sector. It is important that Norway does not introduce a climate policy that contributes to lost industrial opportunities and carbon leakage with subsequent increased global emissions. And we're not going to do that with the government's solution. In the non-quota sector, through an agreement with the EU, Norway will be allocated an emission reduction target of between 0 and 40 pst, based on its gross domestic product per capita and the potential for cost-effective emission reductions. Therefore, there is reason to expect Norway to have a high target of implementing large emission reductions in the transport, construction and agricultural sectors. The potential is greatest in the transport sector, and the government and the cooperation parties are already in the process of implementing comprehensive and reinforced measures to reduce emissions. The target in the non-quota sector is likely to be set in 2016. After this, it will be natural to clarify how we will meet Norway's emission reduction obligations, but I assume that measures to reduce emissions will be promoted continuously in the years ahead and that the government's work already started to reinforce the measures in the climate agreement will continue. As I said, there is a broad consensus on what the government will report to the UN by the end of this month, and there is a broad consensus on what we will try to achieve in cooperation with the EU by 2030. It's very positive. Let me also give the government an honor for the way it has involved Stortinget in this matter. I think it's the first time the Storting has had such a good anchoring of our international climate commitments to the UN. It gives hope that the Storting will also contribute to a good follow-up of the objectives.

This was not the case, but there are some proposals here, and there are some leads from the work that also give us opportunities to discuss the measures in the future. The targets we have now set for the ETS sector mean that one should achieve a 43 pst reduction in CO2 emissions. The centre party believes it is important that not only does one leave the quota-based sector to a quota market, but from the Norwegian side we are actively preparing for the low-carbon industry by continuing measures such as the CO2 tax, but also by positive instruments for technology development in industry. Norway will then, through negotiations with the EU, set a national emission reduction target in the non-quota sector and there is reason to expect Norway to take large emission reductions in this sector. This will mean major shifts in a number of areas. In addition, Norway is free – and I believe that this is reason to stress – to contribute to climate action such as the international forestry effort, the UN Green Fund and the commitment to renewable energy in developing countries. It is a work that should be developed further. One of the sectors facing major changes in the future is the transport sector. A transition to a car park where electric cars and low - emission cars dominate will be absolutely necessary. The country must conclude long-term agreements with the major cities to contribute to more climate-friendly transport solutions. The railway must be further developed, intercity development is important, but the most climate-friendly single measure that you can do within Norwegian rail is electrify the remaining diesel sections, including: Nordlandsbanen, Trønderbanen and Rørosbanen. Forests also play an important role in climate policy. Forest is an example of an area where business development and greenhouse gas reduction go hand in hand, and including the role of forest as carbon stock can simultaneously increase the use of forest resources. Overall, it has been estimated that forests can help reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 2 million tonnes. The majority of the emphasis on the role of forest as carbon stock while the ability of forest to deliver renewable raw materials is very important, and the centre party would certainly have wanted an even clearer statement that a climate agreement needs to stimulate increased use and activity in forests. There's a lot of opportunities ahead of us, and there's a lot of demanding things. The centre party is pleased that there was a broad consensus on the objectives, but we look forward very much to the debate on the further transformation and measures.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e5944	What we are discussing today is what Norway as a nation is committed to doing to ensure that our children and grandchildren will be able to take over a planet that has a viable climate. The Green Party supports, in principle, the approach of the government by joining the EU climate system, with a Norwegian commitment for 2030. This will mean that Norwegian climate targets go from being one of the worst in Europe in practice to getting a lot better. Let us make one thing clear right away: with the constitution of the Committee majority and with the government's proposals, Norway is still not going to cover our legitimate share of global climate responsibility. The main problem that remains is that with the structure proposed by the government and supported by the Christian People's Party and the Left, a policy is pursued that has two major shortcomings. One is that it is tailored to protect Norwegian oil and gas activities from effective climate action, and the other is that it continues to refrain from using other of Norway’s major instruments, which could contribute to global climate change. Since the first Norwegian base oil was extracted in 1969, Norwegian base oil and gas have been incinerated and converted into approximately 14,000 million tonnes of CO2 – 14 billion tonnes of CO2 – in the atmosphere, according to climate researcher Glen Peters at CICERO Climate Research Centre. It's a very large number. It's 280 times more than our national emissions of about 50 million tons. This means that Norway is not a small and unimportant country in the global climate accounts. We are one of those countries that has a real responsibility for global climate change. When we are about to report a new climate obligation to the United Nations, the Green Party believes that an obligation must include an extended thinking on how to discharge Norway’s climate responsibility and how Norway develops its own policy, including industrial policy, in order to contribute to this. Then it is important that we include the major instruments that can be used to solve global climate responsibility. The Greens believe that Norway's commitment to the UN should be based on four pillars. Firstly, we must have a clear target for domestic emissions, because, in line with our historic responsibility as a rich oil country, domestic emissions should be reduced to 20 million tonnes of CO2, corresponding to about 60 pst in 2030. cut compared to 1990 levels. Secondly, we must phase out Norwegian oil and gas production during a 20-year period. It is the production and use of oil, gas and coal that is the climate problem, and at some point you have to assume that it is produced too much and is being looked for too much. Norway has to take part in the responsibility for stepping down that activity. Thirdly, Norway must make a strong contribution to international climate funding. The Green Party has already proposed in Stortinget that we make the corresponding 1 pst by 2020. of gross national income for climate action in developing countries, estimated at 30 billion. kr today. Finally, Norway needs to invest its savings in something that contributes to sustainable development in other countries. That is to say, by 2020, the oil fund should have deducted its investments from fossil investments and put all of them into investments that contribute to sustainable development. So the Greens are in favour of an approach to cooperating with the EU in climate policy, but we note, as the Left and others have also pointed out, that these concrete climate objectives that we are now asking the EU to impose, we have this hall in which to decide. We should have done that, and we should still do that. Today, what will be the concrete impact of the majority climate policy on Norwegian emissions is in blue and blue. The Greens will join the majority's proposals.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e6213	The progress party does not believe the way to solve the climate challenges is by adopting a new law, as the majority advocates. The Progress Party believes that climate and environmental challenges are best managed through concrete actions with the most impact globally as well as nationally. In this context, the Progress Party will refer to the report The New Climate Economy, which was published in the autumn of 2014. The report focuses on opportunities to tackle growth and climate change in three critical systems: cities, agriculture and energy. The three key drivers for change are resource efficiency, infrastructure investment and innovation. The report recommends inter alia: building more productive and cleaner cities, better land use and conversion of renewable energy systems and better resource use. The report distinguishes between measures for low-, medium- and high-income countries. The report is generally very specific in terms of what action should be taken to address the challenges. After the Progress Party's view, the report has been more effective in resolving climate challenges than ever before. The Progress Party questions the purpose of the laws, and not least the way in which the laws are to be enforced. Climate challenges are a global problem that is not solved by national prohibition laws, but through international agreements that oblige the parties to take action to address climate and environmental challenges. The party of progress is afraid that a climate law will become a paper tiger: at first sight, it will seem daring, but in reality it will be toothless and without claws. A law marks high time, authority, and importance, but is no guarantee of goal attainment, as many people seem to believe. If it was that simple, there were several themes that suggested a law – perhaps a law against empty swimming pools, a law against poverty for children, or a law against involuntary part-time, and so on. There are many important political issues that are not solved, and if we are to solve them by law, we have to be sure to put those laws in place. The party of progress thinks we're undermining the rule of law by laws that don't actually help solve the challenges. ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e6232	If one does not fully agree with one government party, it is good to be comforted by the fact that the second government party had a very good post, because the post of representative Tina Bru made me think that the Christian People's Party has been a strong driver of climate law for a long time, and at the last turn in the climate negotiations we got it in, and then the appropriateness had to be investigated. I remember that in one of the last posts I had before the 2013 elections, I asked the then Environment Minister Bård Vegar Solhell if he had followed it up. It had happened the year before, and nothing had been started. As the representative Tina Bru came up with, it appeared in a look-up later that he was strongly opposed to it. That is why it was important for us, together with the Greens/Centre Party, to promote the proposal here because we felt it was urgent. That's why I'm very happy that there's become a majority in this case. The United Kingdom, as I have shown many times, has good experience with its climate legislation, which was adopted in 2008. The British law legislates 80 pst. reductions in greenhouse gas emissions up to 2050, by 1990 as reference year. All the departments are obliged to follow their allocated carbon budget, and therefore I would like to tell the representative Grimstad that one will thus also be obliged by the law to follow up. That is why we believe that this will be an important instrument. They see it as a good tool for creating long-term binding policy solutions, and it has strengthened the influence of the Ministry of Climate and Energy on government. The United Kingdom has also significantly changed its energy policy since the law was passed on to them. Several other countries have, or are in the process of implementing, national climate laws. In 2012, Mexico followed and then Finland and Denmark, and China is working on legislative proposals. There is a great reason to be enthusiastic about the fact that almost all of the parties are now in favour of Norway being given its climate legislation during this majorisation period. It is absolutely essential that the consensus we have on this proposal results in a strong and binding climate law. We believe it is absolutely essential that national climate targets are based on facts from climate science. There is broad political agreement in Norway on many of the measures to achieve the 2 degree target, but many of the main measures needed can be rapidly prioritised in competition with more short-term measures. A climate law with carbon budget according to the British model will ensure a longer-term climate policy. It will shift the discussion from how, because we set the framework, but leaves to politicians to decide the measures to reduce emissions sufficiently. A climate law will oblige any government to follow up climate objectives and provide tools for systematic reporting of actions undertaken. Today, we lack an overall tool to provide long-term emission reductions in all sectors. A climate law will make the overall emission targets legally binding. The WWF, which has been in the process of implementing climate legislation in the last five years, has pointed to four elements in order to ensure a strong climate law: Numerous national emission targets for 2030 and 2050, which are in line with Norway’s responsibility to avoid 2 degrees of global warming – we did so in the previous case. Carbon budgets that allocate Norwegian emissions reductions to all sectors, so that we ensure a predictable business environment and underpin any decisions to be taken in the administration. Detailed and sectoral reporting in the annual government budgets showing how the sum of planned emissions and emission reduction measures ensures a trend in line with the adopted climate targets. A secretariat responsible for making professional recommendations to the government, including the preparation of the carbon budget and the annual reporting to the Stortinget. We believe these are important points that the government has to look at when to follow it up, and a climate law according to that pattern, and according to the British law, is one of the most important laws that Stortinget can adopt, because we believe it will ensure a longer-term climate policy.

In other words, we have to make it clear that there is the amount of greenhouse gas measured in tonnes of CO2 equivalent that Norway will release in 2030 and 2050, which is what this is about. It's what matters how much greenhouse gas emits into the atmosphere. Secondly, the Climate Act must break down this overall national target on specific targets and carbon budgets for each department or sector, and each State Council must have a carbon budget on which it is obliged to deliver. This means that the carbon budgets can be managed, for example: over four years, term of office. ParlaMint-NO_2015-03-24.ana.ud2153e6348	As the previous case has shown well tonight, the government is pursuing an active and planned climate policy, where we are constantly taking new steps to further develop Norwegian climate policy. I thought it was important to invite widely to the views of key social actors and businesses, before the government finally took a position as to whether it would initiate a legislative work. Sending a case on consultation does not often happen in Norway, but it is a very common step in some other countries and in the EU's internal decision-making processes. The note ‘Perspectives of a climate law’ was sent at a wide consultation last autumn. There has also been a discussion with the Climate Council, with representatives from businesses, organisations, research and management, and an open consultation meeting has been held in the Ministry of Climate and Environment. The case has proved to be involved. I received a wide range of views, and more than 13 000 support signatures, through this consultation round before it was concluded in January. The main emphasis of those who have spoken is predominantly positive to climate law, or that it deserves to be further investigated. I believe that this is important, because the starting point is that we already have strong legislation in Norway, where, in particular, the pollution law combined with the climate quota law is a cornerstone and is a solid basis of law to control greenhouse gas emissions through instruments anchored in law.
